```python
health_report = monitor.check_health(recent_window=20)

# Create monitoring dashboard
monitor.plot_monitoring_dashboard()
```

# Section 7.3: MLOps for Time Series Forecasting

## Learning Objectives

By the end of this section, students should understand how to build production-ready time series forecasting systems. Unlike traditional machine learning models that can be deployed and left relatively static, time series models operate in a constantly changing environment where the underlying data distribution evolves over time. Students will learn to design automated retraining pipelines that detect when models degrade, implement proper versioning strategies that maintain reproducibility, and conduct statistically sound A/B tests that account for temporal dependencies. The key insight is that time series models require continuous monitoring and adaptation because the future rarely looks exactly like the past.

## 7.3.1 Automated Retraining Strategies

### The Problem: When Should We Retrain?

Time series models face a fundamental challenge that distinguishes them from other machine learning applications. The statistical properties of the data generating process can change over time, a phenomenon called concept drift or non-stationarity. A sales forecasting model trained on pre-pandemic data would perform poorly during COVID-19 lockdowns, not because the model is fundamentally flawed, but because customer behavior fundamentally changed. The question then becomes: how do we detect these changes and respond appropriately?

There are three primary approaches to automated retraining, each with distinct trade-offs. The scheduled retraining approach retrains models at fixed intervals, such as weekly or monthly. This is the simplest strategy to implement and provides predictable computational costs, but it can be wasteful during stable periods and dangerously slow during rapid changes. The performance-triggered approach monitors forecast accuracy metrics and retrains when performance degrades beyond a threshold. This is more adaptive but requires careful threshold selection and can be sensitive to natural accuracy fluctuations. The drift-detection approach uses statistical tests to detect changes in the underlying data distribution and triggers retraining proactively before performance degrades significantly.

### Mathematical Framework for Drift Detection

The core mathematical question is whether the current data follows the same distribution as the training data. We formalize this using statistical hypothesis testing. Let P_train represent the probability distribution of our training data and P_current represent the distribution of recent observations. Our null hypothesis H_0 states that P_train equals P_current, meaning no drift has occurred. The alternative hypothesis H_1 states that these distributions differ.

The Page-Hinkley test provides an elegant online algorithm for detecting distribution changes. It maintains a cumulative sum of deviations from the mean and triggers an alarm when this sum exceeds a threshold. The test statistic at time t is defined as m_t equals the sum from i=1 to t of the quantity (x_i minus x_bar minus delta), where x_bar is the mean of observed values and delta is a magnitude parameter controlling sensitivity. We detect drift when m_t minus the minimum of m_t exceeds lambda, our detection threshold.

In Python, we can implement this as follows:

```python
import numpy as np

class PageHinkleyDriftDetector:
    """
    Detects concept drift using the Page-Hinkley test.
    
    The test maintains a cumulative sum of deviations and triggers
    when this sum exceeds a threshold, indicating the mean has shifted.
    """
    def __init__(self, delta=0.005, threshold=50, direction='both'):
        # delta: magnitude of change to detect (sensitivity parameter)
        # threshold: alarm threshold (higher = less sensitive)
        # direction: 'increase', 'decrease', or 'both'
        self.delta = delta
        self.threshold = threshold
        self.direction = direction
        self.reset()
    
    def reset(self):
        """Initialize or reset the detector state."""
        self.sum = 0
        self.x_mean = 0
        self.n = 0
        self.min_sum = 0
        self.max_sum = 0
        
    def update(self, value):
        """
        Update detector with new observation.
        Returns True if drift detected.
        """
        # Update running mean incrementally
        self.n += 1
        self.x_mean += (value - self.x_mean) / self.n
        
        # Update cumulative sum with deviation from mean
        self.sum += value - self.x_mean - self.delta
        
        # Track minimum and maximum for bidirectional detection
        self.min_sum = min(self.min_sum, self.sum)
        self.max_sum = max(self.max_sum, self.sum)
        
        # Check for drift based on direction
        if self.direction in ['increase', 'both']:
            if self.sum - self.min_sum > self.threshold:
                return True
        
        if self.direction in ['decrease', 'both']:
            if self.max_sum - self.sum > self.threshold:
                return True
                
        return False
```

The ADWIN (Adaptive Windowing) algorithm offers a more sophisticated approach that automatically adjusts its window size based on detected changes. It maintains a sliding window of recent observations and uses a statistical test to determine if the mean of the left subwindow differs significantly from the right subwindow. When a significant difference is detected, older data is dropped and the window size contracts.

### Performance Monitoring and Degradation Detection

Beyond distribution drift, we need to monitor actual forecast performance. The challenge is distinguishing between natural performance fluctuations and genuine model degradation. We compute rolling forecast errors using a sliding window approach. For a window size w, we calculate the Mean Absolute Error over the last w predictions: MAE_t equals the average from i equals t minus w plus one to t of the absolute value of y_i minus y_hat_i.

We establish control limits using historical performance during the validation period. If we assume forecast errors follow approximately a normal distribution, we can set upper and lower control limits using the mean plus or minus z times the standard deviation, where z corresponds to our desired confidence level. Common choices are z equals two for ninety-five percent confidence or z equals three for ninety-nine point seven percent confidence. When the rolling MAE exceeds the upper control limit for several consecutive periods, we trigger a retraining event.

```python
import pandas as pd
from collections import deque

class ForecastMonitor:
    """
    Monitors forecast performance and detects degradation.
    
    Uses rolling error metrics with statistical control limits
    to identify when model performance has degraded significantly.
    """
    def __init__(self, window_size=30, n_std=2, alert_consecutive=3):
        # window_size: number of recent predictions to monitor
        # n_std: number of standard deviations for control limits
        # alert_consecutive: consecutive violations before alerting
        self.window_size = window_size
        self.n_std = n_std
        self.alert_consecutive = alert_consecutive
        self.errors = deque(maxlen=window_size)
        self.consecutive_violations = 0
        
        # Performance metrics from validation period
        self.baseline_mae = None
        self.baseline_std = None
        self.upper_control_limit = None
        
    def set_baseline(self, validation_errors):
        """
        Establish control limits from validation period errors.
        These define 'normal' performance expectations.
        """
        self.baseline_mae = np.mean(np.abs(validation_errors))
        self.baseline_std = np.std(np.abs(validation_errors))
        self.upper_control_limit = self.baseline_mae + self.n_std * self.baseline_std
        
    def update(self, actual, predicted):
        """
        Add new prediction and check if retraining needed.
        Returns (should_retrain, current_mae, alert_message)
        """
        error = abs(actual - predicted)
        self.errors.append(error)
        
        if len(self.errors) < self.window_size:
            return False, None, "Collecting initial observations"
        
        current_mae = np.mean(self.errors)
        
        # Check if current performance exceeds control limit
        if current_mae > self.upper_control_limit:
            self.consecutive_violations += 1
            if self.consecutive_violations >= self.alert_consecutive:
                message = f"Performance degraded: MAE {current_mae:.3f} > UCL {self.upper_control_limit:.3f}"
                return True, current_mae, message
        else:
            # Reset counter when performance returns to normal
            self.consecutive_violations = 0
            
        return False, current_mae, "Performance within normal range"
```

### Hybrid Retraining Strategies

In practice, the most robust systems combine multiple triggers. A typical production system might use a minimum retraining frequency of monthly to ensure the model stays reasonably current, performance-based triggers that initiate retraining when accuracy degrades beyond acceptable thresholds, and drift detection that provides early warning before performance impacts become visible. This multi-layered approach balances computational efficiency with responsiveness to changing conditions.

The implementation requires careful consideration of the retraining window. When we detect drift or performance degradation, we must decide how much historical data to include in retraining. Using all available data provides maximum sample size but may include obsolete patterns. Using only recent data adapts quickly to new patterns but may overfit to short-term fluctuations. An exponentially weighted moving window provides a middle ground, giving higher weight to recent observations while retaining some historical context.

## 7.3.2 Model Versioning and Registry

### The Reproducibility Challenge

In production time series systems, reproducibility is not merely a convenience but a critical requirement for debugging, auditing, and regulatory compliance. When a forecast influences a business decision that later proves problematic, we need the ability to exactly recreate what the model predicted and why. However, time series models introduce unique versioning challenges. Unlike image classification models where the training set is fixed, time series models are trained on data that grows over time. The model trained on January first differs from the model trained on February first not just in parameters but in the fundamental scope of training data.

### What to Version

A complete model version must capture six essential elements. The model artifacts include the trained parameters, architecture specification, and any preprocessing transformers or scalers. The code includes the exact version of the training script, dependency versions for libraries like scikit-learn or prophet, and any custom feature engineering functions. The data encompasses not just the final training set but also the data collection timestamps, preprocessing steps applied, and train-validation-test split indices. The hyperparameters include all configuration used during training, from learning rates to seasonal period specifications. The metadata captures training duration, hardware specifications, and the person or system that initiated training. Finally, the performance metrics document validation accuracy, cross-validation results, and confidence intervals.

### Implementing a Model Registry

A model registry serves as a central repository tracking all model versions and their metadata. The structure typically follows a hierarchical organization where each model family, such as daily sales forecasts, contains multiple versions, each identified by timestamp or sequential version number. For each version, we store the model binary, configuration file, training data reference, performance metrics, and deployment status.

```python
import mlflow
import pandas as pd
from datetime import datetime
import json

class TimeSeriesModelRegistry:
    """
    Manages versioning and metadata for time series forecasting models.
    
    Integrates with MLflow for artifact storage and tracking,
    with additional time series-specific metadata.
    """
    def __init__(self, registry_uri, experiment_name):
        mlflow.set_tracking_uri(registry_uri)
        mlflow.set_experiment(experiment_name)
        self.experiment_name = experiment_name
        
    def register_model(self, model, model_name, train_data, 
                       validation_data, config, metrics):
        """
        Register a new model version with complete metadata.
        
        Args:
            model: Trained model object (must be picklable)
            model_name: Name for this model family
            train_data: Training dataset with temporal info
            validation_data: Validation dataset
            config: Dictionary of hyperparameters and settings
            metrics: Dictionary of performance metrics
        """
        with mlflow.start_run():
            # Log model artifacts
            mlflow.sklearn.log_model(model, "model")
            
            # Log hyperparameters
            mlflow.log_params(config)
            
            # Log performance metrics
            mlflow.log_metrics(metrics)
            
            # Log time series specific metadata
            ts_metadata = {
                "train_start": str(train_data.index.min()),
                "train_end": str(train_data.index.max()),
                "train_samples": len(train_data),
                "validation_start": str(validation_data.index.min()),
                "validation_end": str(validation_data.index.max()),
                "frequency": self._infer_frequency(train_data.index),
                "features": list(train_data.columns),
                "target": config.get("target_variable"),
                "registration_time": datetime.now().isoformat()
            }
            
            # Save metadata as artifact
            metadata_path = "ts_metadata.json"
            with open(metadata_path, 'w') as f:
                json.dump(ts_metadata, f, indent=2)
            mlflow.log_artifact(metadata_path)
            
            # Register model with MLflow Model Registry
            run_id = mlflow.active_run().info.run_id
            model_uri = f"runs:/{run_id}/model"
            model_details = mlflow.register_model(model_uri, model_name)
            
            return model_details.version
            
    def _infer_frequency(self, index):
        """Infer time series frequency from datetime index."""
        if not isinstance(index, pd.DatetimeIndex):
            return "unknown"
        inferred = pd.infer_freq(index)
        return inferred if inferred else "irregular"
    
    def load_model_version(self, model_name, version=None, stage=None):
        """
        Load specific model version or stage (e.g., 'Production').
        
        Returns tuple of (model, metadata)
        """
        if stage:
            model_uri = f"models:/{model_name}/{stage}"
        elif version:
            model_uri = f"models:/{model_name}/{version}"
        else:
            raise ValueError("Must specify either version or stage")
            
        # Load model
        model = mlflow.sklearn.load_model(model_uri)
        
        # Load metadata
        client = mlflow.tracking.MlflowClient()
        if stage:
            versions = client.get_latest_versions(model_name, stages=[stage])
            run_id = versions[0].run_id
        else:
            model_version = client.get_model_version(model_name, version)
            run_id = model_version.run_id
            
        # Download and load metadata
        artifact_path = client.download_artifacts(run_id, "ts_metadata.json")
        with open(artifact_path, 'r') as f:
            metadata = json.load(f)
            
        return model, metadata
    
    def compare_versions(self, model_name, version1, version2):
        """
        Compare two model versions across key metrics and metadata.
        Useful for understanding model evolution over time.
        """
        client = mlflow.tracking.MlflowClient()
        
        def get_version_info(version):
            mv = client.get_model_version(model_name, version)
            run = client.get_run(mv.run_id)
            return {
                "version": version,
                "metrics": run.data.metrics,
                "params": run.data.params,
                "created": mv.creation_timestamp
            }
        
        v1_info = get_version_info(version1)
        v2_info = get_version_info(version2)
        
        comparison = {
            "metric_changes": {},
            "param_changes": {},
            "time_difference_days": (v2_info["created"] - v1_info["created"]) / (1000 * 86400)
        }
        
        # Compare metrics
        for metric in v1_info["metrics"]:
            if metric in v2_info["metrics"]:
                old_val = v1_info["metrics"][metric]
                new_val = v2_info["metrics"][metric]
                comparison["metric_changes"][metric] = {
                    "old": old_val,
                    "new": new_val,
                    "change": new_val - old_val,
                    "pct_change": ((new_val - old_val) / old_val * 100) if old_val != 0 else None
                }
        
        return comparison
```

### Data Versioning for Time Series

Time series data versioning requires special consideration because the data grows continuously. Rather than versioning entire datasets, we version data snapshots that correspond to model training events. Each snapshot records the query or filter used to extract training data, the timestamp when data was extracted, any preprocessing transformations applied, and checksums to verify data integrity.

For large datasets, we often version the data extraction code and parameters rather than the data itself. This approach stores the SQL queries or API calls used to retrieve data along with the timestamp ranges extracted. This ensures reproducibility while managing storage costs. When we need to recreate a model, we can re-extract the data using the stored queries, understanding that the underlying database might have been corrected or updated since the original extraction.

## 7.3.3 A/B Testing in Production

### Unique Challenges for Time Series A/B Testing

A/B testing forecasting models presents fundamentally different challenges than testing user interface changes or recommendation algorithms. Traditional A/B tests assume independent observations where showing user A one experience and user B another experience does not violate any statistical assumptions. Time series forecasts, however, are inherently dependent across time. We cannot randomly assign different forecasts to different time points because business decisions often depend on forecast consistency and the forecasts themselves may influence future outcomes.

The temporal correlation problem arises because forecast errors at time t are often correlated with errors at time t plus one. If model A happens to encounter an unusual week during its testing period, this does not necessarily mean model A is worse overall. The feedback loop problem occurs when forecasts influence decisions that affect future actuals. For example, if an inventory forecast is too high, we order more stock, which might lead to promotional pricing that affects future sales patterns. The sample size limitation emerges because we cannot simply run tests longer to gather more data. Each day that passes changes the underlying data distribution, so testing for six months does not give us six times as much information as testing for one month.

### Interleaved Testing Approach

One solution is interleaved testing, where we switch between models at regular intervals and carefully account for temporal effects. We might use model A for week one, model B for week two, model A for week three, and so forth. This ensures both models experience similar temporal conditions. The key is to model the time effects explicitly in our analysis.

```python
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from scipy import stats
import statsmodels.api as sm

class TimeSeriesABTest:
    """
    Conducts statistically valid A/B tests for time series forecasts.
    
    Handles temporal correlation and switching between models
    to ensure fair comparison under similar conditions.
    """
    def __init__(self, metric='mae', confidence_level=0.95):
        self.metric = metric
        self.confidence_level = confidence_level
        self.results = []
        
    def add_observation(self, timestamp, model_name, actual, predicted):
        """Record a forecast and its actual outcome."""
        error = actual - predicted
        abs_error = abs(error)
        squared_error = error ** 2
        
        self.results.append({
            'timestamp': timestamp,
            'model': model_name,
            'actual': actual,
            'predicted': predicted,
            'error': error,
            'abs_error': abs_error,
            'squared_error': squared_error
        })
    
    def analyze_with_temporal_controls(self):
        """
        Analyze test results accounting for temporal effects.
        
        Uses regression with time controls to isolate model effect
        from natural time series trends and seasonality.
        """
        df = pd.DataFrame(self.results)
        df['timestamp'] = pd.to_datetime(df['timestamp'])
        df = df.sort_values('timestamp')
        
        # Create time-based features to control for temporal effects
        df['day_of_week'] = df['timestamp'].dt.dayofweek
        df['month'] = df['timestamp'].dt.month
        df['time_index'] = range(len(df))  # Linear time trend
        
        # Create model indicator (1 for model B, 0 for model A)
        df['is_model_b'] = (df['model'] == 'B').astype(int)
        
        # Select metric
        if self.metric == 'mae':
            y = df['abs_error']
        elif self.metric == 'mse':
            y = df['squared_error']
        else:
            y = df['error']
        
        # Build regression model controlling for temporal effects
        # The coefficient on is_model_b tells us the model effect
        # after controlling for day of week, month, and trend
        X = pd.get_dummies(df[['is_model_b', 'day_of_week', 'month', 'time_index']], 
                          columns=['day_of_week', 'month'], drop_first=True)
        
        # Add constant term
        X = sm.add_constant(X)
        
        # Fit OLS regression
        model = sm.OLS(y, X).fit()
        
        # Extract model effect (coefficient on is_model_b)
        model_effect = model.params['is_model_b']
        model_effect_se = model.bse['is_model_b']
        t_stat = model.tvalues['is_model_b']
        p_value = model.pvalues['is_model_b']
        
        # Calculate confidence interval for model effect
        critical_value = stats.t.ppf((1 + self.confidence_level) / 2, model.df_resid)
        ci_lower = model_effect - critical_value * model_effect_se
        ci_upper = model_effect + critical_value * model_effect_se
        
        # Compute average metrics per model for interpretation
        model_a_avg = df[df['model'] == 'A'][y.name].mean()
        model_b_avg = df[df['model'] == 'B'][y.name].mean()
        
        return {
            'model_a_avg': model_a_avg,
            'model_b_avg': model_b_avg,
            'raw_difference': model_b_avg - model_a_avg,
            'controlled_effect': model_effect,
            'standard_error': model_effect_se,
            'confidence_interval': (ci_lower, ci_upper),
            't_statistic': t_stat,
            'p_value': p_value,
            'significant': p_value < (1 - self.confidence_level),
            'winner': 'Model B' if model_effect < 0 else 'Model A' if model_effect > 0 else 'Tie'
        }
    
    def holdout_validation(self, model_a, model_b, test_data):
        """
        Alternative approach: Both models forecast the same holdout period.
        
        This avoids temporal confounding but requires waiting for
        actuals to materialize before making decisions.
        """
        results_a = []
        results_b = []
        
        for idx in range(len(test_data)):
            actual = test_data.iloc[idx]['target']
            
            # Get predictions from both models
            pred_a = model_a.predict(test_data.iloc[idx:idx+1])[0]
            pred_b = model_b.predict(test_data.iloc[idx:idx+1])[0]
            
            results_a.append(abs(actual - pred_a))
            results_b.append(abs(actual - pred_b))
        
        # Paired t-test since same time periods
        t_stat, p_value = stats.ttest_rel(results_a, results_b)
        
        mean_a = np.mean(results_a)
        mean_b = np.mean(results_b)
        diff = mean_b - mean_a
        
        return {
            'model_a_mae': mean_a,
            'model_b_mae': mean_b,
            'difference': diff,
            't_statistic': t_stat,
            'p_value': p_value,
            'significant': p_value < (1 - self.confidence_level),
            'winner': 'Model B' if diff < 0 else 'Model A' if diff > 0 else 'Tie'
        }
```

### Sequential Testing and Early Stopping

For situations where we need faster decisions, sequential testing methods allow us to monitor the test continuously and stop early when sufficient evidence accumulates. The Sequential Probability Ratio Test computes a likelihood ratio at each time step and stops when this ratio exceeds thresholds corresponding to our desired Type I and Type II error rates.

The approach computes the log-likelihood ratio: lambda_t equals the sum from i equals one to t of log of the probability of observation y_i under hypothesis H_1 divided by the probability under hypothesis H_0. We stop and reject H_0 (conclude model B is better) when lambda_t exceeds log of the quantity one minus beta divided by alpha, or we stop and accept H_0 (conclude model A is better) when lambda_t falls below log of the quantity beta divided by one minus alpha. Here alpha represents our acceptable false positive rate and beta represents our acceptable false negative rate.

### Practical Guidelines for Time Series A/B Testing

In practice, successful A/B testing of forecasting models requires careful experimental design. We should run tests for complete seasonal cycles to ensure both models experience all seasonal patterns. A daily model should run for at least several weeks to capture weekly seasonality. We must define clear success metrics before starting the test, including primary metrics like MAE or MAPE and secondary metrics such as bias, calibration, or business impact measures.

We should implement monitoring for data quality issues during the test period, as unusual data patterns can invalidate results. The test should include provisions for early stopping if one model performs dramatically worse, protecting against excessive business impact. Finally, we must document the test protocol, including randomization scheme, temporal controls applied, and any anomalies observed during the test period.

---

# Section 8: Advanced Topics in Time Series Forecasting

## Learning Objectives

This section covers sophisticated forecasting methods that address complex real-world scenarios. Students will understand the mathematical foundations of ensemble learning and how to properly combine multiple forecasts to achieve better accuracy than any individual model. They will learn hierarchical forecasting techniques that ensure forecasts remain coherent across organizational levels, from national totals down to individual stores. The section covers probabilistic forecasting methods that quantify uncertainty and provide prediction intervals with guaranteed coverage. Finally, students will understand how to move beyond mere correlation to identify causal relationships in time series data, distinguishing between spurious associations and genuine causal effects.

## 8.1 Ensemble Methods for Time Series

### Why Ensembles Work: The Bias-Variance Decomposition

The power of ensemble methods rests on a fundamental statistical principle. When we make a prediction, our error can be decomposed into three components: bias (systematic error from incorrect assumptions), variance (error from sensitivity to training data fluctuations), and irreducible error (inherent noise in the data). Individual models often trade off between bias and variance. Complex models like deep neural networks have low bias but high variance, while simple models like linear regression have low variance but may have high bias.

Ensembles reduce variance without increasing bias by combining predictions from multiple models. If we have M independent models, each with variance sigma squared, and we average their predictions, the variance of the ensemble is sigma squared divided by M. This is the same principle that makes polling more accurate when you survey more people. However, the key word is independent. If all models make similar errors, averaging provides no benefit.

### Diversity in Ensemble Construction

Creating effective ensembles requires ensuring diversity among member models. We can achieve diversity through several mechanisms. Different algorithms naturally make different types of errors; combining ARIMA, Prophet, and XGBoost leverages their complementary strengths. Different training periods allow models to specialize on different temporal patterns; one model might capture recent trends while another captures long-term seasonality. Different features enable models to focus on different aspects of the problem; one model might use calendar features while another emphasizes lagged values. Finally, different hyperparameters cause the same algorithm to make different predictions.

### Simple Averaging and Weighted Averaging

The simplest ensemble method averages predictions from all models: y_hat_ensemble equals one over M times the sum from i equals one to M of y_hat_i, where y_hat_i is the prediction from model i. This works surprisingly well and is robust to poor model selection, as no single model can dominate the ensemble.

Weighted averaging assigns different weights to different models based on their historical performance: y_hat_ensemble equals the sum from i equals one to M of w_i times y_hat_i, where the weights w_i sum to one. The challenge lies in estimating weights that generalize to future data rather than overfitting to validation data.

```python
import numpy as np
from sklearn.linear_model import Ridge
from sklearn.metrics import mean_absolute_error

class TimeSeriesEnsemble:
    """
    Ensemble forecasting with multiple combining strategies.
    
    Implements simple averaging, weighted averaging, and
    stacking with cross-validation to prevent overfitting.
    """
    def __init__(self, models, method='simple_average'):
        """
        Args:
            models: List of (name, model) tuples
            method: 'simple_average', 'weighted_average', 'stacking', 'best_model'
        """
        self.models = models
        self.method = method
        self.weights = None
        self.meta_model = None
        
    def fit_weights(self, X_val, y_val):
        """
        Learn weights by minimizing validation error.
        
        Uses constrained optimization to find weights that
        sum to 1 and minimize ensemble error on validation set.
        """
        # Get predictions from all models on validation set
        predictions = np.column_stack([
            model.predict(X_val) for name, model in self.models
        ])
        
        if self.method == 'simple_average':
            # Equal weights
            self.weights = np.ones(len(self.models)) / len(self.models)
            
        elif self.method == 'weighted_average':
            # Weight by inverse validation error
            errors = np.array([
                mean_absolute_error(y_val, pred) 
                for pred in predictions.T
            ])
            # Inverse error weighting (smaller error = higher weight)
            inv_errors = 1 / (errors + 1e-10)  # avoid division by zero
            self.weights = inv_errors / inv_errors.sum()
            
        elif self.method == 'stacking':
            # Learn weights using ridge regression
            # Ridge prevents overfitting to validation set
            self.meta_model = Ridge(alpha=1.0, fit_intercept=False)
            
            # Constrain weights to be non-negative using positive feature constraint
            # We'll use a simple approach: fit Ridge and clip negative weights
            self.meta_model.fit(predictions, y_val)
            raw_weights = self.meta_model.coef_
            # Clip and renormalize
            self.weights = np.maximum(raw_weights, 0)
            if self.weights.sum() > 0:
                self.weights = self.weights / self.weights.sum()
            else:
                # Fallback to equal weights if all negative
                self.weights = np.ones(len(self.models)) / len(self.models)
                
        elif self.method == 'best_model':
            # Select single best model (not really an ensemble)
            errors = np.array([
                mean_absolute_error(y_val, pred) 
                for pred in predictions.T
            ])
            best_idx = np.argmin(errors)
            self.weights = np.zeros(len(self.models))
            self.weights[best_idx] = 1.0
    
    def predict(self, X):
        """Generate ensemble forecast."""
        # Get predictions from all models
        predictions = np.column_stack([
            model.predict(X) for name, model in self.models
        ])
        
        if self.weights is None:
            raise ValueError("Must call fit_weights before predict")
        
        # Combine using learned weights
        ensemble_pred = predictions @ self.weights
        
        return ensemble_pred
    
    def get_model_contributions(self, X):
        """
        Show how much each model contributes to final prediction.
        Useful for interpretability and debugging.
        """
        predictions = np.column_stack([
            model.predict(X) for name, model in self.models
        ])
        
        contributions = predictions * self.weights
        
        return pd.DataFrame({
            'model': [name for name, _ in self.models],
            'weight': self.weights,
            'prediction': predictions.mean(axis=0),
            'contribution': contributions.mean(axis=0)
        })
```

### Stacking: Meta-Learning for Ensembles

Stacking takes ensemble learning a step further by training a meta-model to combine base model predictions. Instead of using fixed weights, we treat base model predictions as features and train a second-level model to learn the optimal combination. The meta-model could be a simple linear regression, a ridge regression for regularization, or even a gradient boosted model for complex nonlinear combinations.

The critical challenge is preventing overfitting. If we train the meta-model on the same data used to train base models, it will learn to exploit base model errors on training data rather than learning genuine complementary patterns. We address this through cross-validation. We split the data into K folds, train each base model on K minus one folds, generate predictions on the held-out fold, and repeat for all folds. This creates out-of-sample predictions for the entire training set, which we then use to train the meta-model.

```python
from sklearn.model_selection import TimeSeriesSplit

class StackingEnsemble:
    """
    Stacking ensemble with cross-validation to prevent overfitting.
    
    Uses time series cross-validation to generate out-of-sample
    predictions from base models, then trains meta-model on these.
    """
    def __init__(self, base_models, meta_model, n_splits=5):
        """
        Args:
            base_models: List of (name, model) tuples for base layer
            meta_model: Model for combining base predictions
            n_splits: Number of cross-validation folds
        """
        self.base_models = base_models
        self.meta_model = meta_model
        self.n_splits = n_splits
        self.fitted_base_models = []
        
    def fit(self, X, y):
        """
        Train ensemble using stacked generalization.
        
        Uses time series cross-validation to generate training
        data for meta-model without overfitting.
        """
        # Use time series cross-validation to maintain temporal order
        tscv = TimeSeriesSplit(n_splits=self.n_splits)
        
        # Store out-of-fold predictions for meta-model training
        oof_predictions = np.zeros((len(X), len(self.base_models)))
        
        # Train each base model using cross-validation
        for model_idx, (name, model) in enumerate(self.base_models):
            fold_predictions = np.zeros(len(X))
            
            for train_idx, val_idx in tscv.split(X):
                # Split data maintaining temporal order
                X_train_fold = X[train_idx]
                y_train_fold = y[train_idx]
                X_val_fold = X[val_idx]
                
                # Train model on this fold
                fold_model = clone_model(model)
                fold_model.fit(X_train_fold, y_train_fold)
                
                # Predict on held-out fold (out-of-fold predictions)
                fold_predictions[val_idx] = fold_model.predict(X_val_fold)
            
            # Store out-of-fold predictions
            oof_predictions[:, model_idx] = fold_predictions
            
            # Train final base model on all data
            final_model = clone_model(model)
            final_model.fit(X, y)
            self.fitted_base_models.append((name, final_model))
        
        # Train meta-model on out-of-fold predictions
        # These are unbiased because each prediction was made on data
        # not used to train that model
        self.meta_model.fit(oof_predictions, y)
        
        return self
    
    def predict(self, X):
        """Generate predictions using stacked ensemble."""
        # Get predictions from all fitted base models
        base_predictions = np.column_stack([
            model.predict(X) for name, model in self.fitted_base_models
        ])
        
        # Meta-model combines base predictions
        ensemble_prediction = self.meta_model.predict(base_predictions)
        
        return ensemble_prediction

def clone_model(model):
    """Create a fresh copy of model with same hyperparameters."""
    from sklearn.base import clone
    return clone(model)
```

### Theoretical Analysis: When Does Stacking Help?

Stacking provides the most benefit when base models make different types of errors. Mathematically, if we have two models with predictions f_1 and f_2, the correlation between their errors rho_error determines the ensemble benefit. If errors are perfectly correlated (rho_error equals one), averaging provides no variance reduction. If errors are uncorrelated (rho_error equals zero), we get the maximum variance reduction.

We can formalize this through the covariance structure of predictions. For an ensemble of M models with equal variance sigma squared and pairwise correlation rho between errors, the variance of the simple average is: Var of y_hat_ensemble equals sigma squared times the quantity one plus the quantity M minus one times rho all divided by M. As M increases, this approaches sigma squared times rho for large M. This shows that reducing correlation between models is as important as adding more models.

## 8.2 Hierarchical Forecasting with Reconciliation

### The Coherence Problem

Many organizations need forecasts at multiple levels of aggregation simultaneously. A retailer might need forecasts for total company sales, sales by region, sales by product category, and sales by individual store-product combinations. These forecasts must be coherent, meaning that if we sum up store-level forecasts for a region, they should equal the regional forecast. If we independently forecast each level, this coherence is almost never satisfied naturally.

The mathematical structure is a hierarchy or grouped structure. We can represent this with a summing matrix S. If we have n bottom-level series and m total series (including aggregates), then S is an m by n matrix where each row represents how to aggregate bottom-level series to form that aggregate. For example, if we have two stores (bottom level) and want to forecast their total (top level), S would be:

Total = [1, 1] times [Store 1, Store 2] transpose

The coherence constraint requires that y_hat equals S times b_hat, where y_hat contains all forecasts and b_hat contains bottom-level forecasts. Any forecast that satisfies this equation is called coherent.

### Bottom-Up Forecasting

The simplest approach to coherent forecasting is bottom-up: forecast the most disaggregated series and sum them to create forecasts for all higher levels. This automatically ensures coherence because we construct aggregates using the summing matrix. The advantage is simplicity and guaranteed coherence. The disadvantage is that we ignore potentially useful information at aggregate levels, and bottom-level series often have high noise and limited sample sizes, leading to poor forecasts that propagate upward.

### Top-Down Forecasting

Top-down forecasting takes the opposite approach: forecast the total and distribute it across lower levels using historical proportions. If we forecast total sales of one hundred units and historically sixty percent went to region A and forty percent to region B, we allocate sixty units to region A and forty to region B. This uses more stable aggregate data but imposes strong assumptions about fixed proportions and cannot capture local trends that differ from the aggregate.

### Optimal Reconciliation: The MinT Approach

Modern hierarchical forecasting uses reconciliation, which generates base forecasts at all levels independently, then adjusts them to be coherent while minimizing forecast error. The key insight is that base forecasts contain useful information even though they are incoherent, so we should not discard them entirely.

The reconciliation process produces a coherent forecast y_tilde that is close to the original base forecast y_hat in a weighted least squares sense. We solve: minimize over b the quantity (y_hat minus S b) transpose times W times (y_hat minus S b), where W is a weight matrix. The solution is y_tilde equals S times (S transpose W S) inverse times S transpose times W times y_hat.

The choice of weight matrix W encodes our beliefs about relative forecast accuracy at different levels. The Ordinary Least Squares reconciliation uses W equals the identity matrix, treating all forecast errors equally. The Weighted Least Squares reconciliation uses W equals the diagonal matrix of historical forecast variances, giving less weight to less accurate forecasts. The Minimum Trace or MinT reconciliation uses W equals the estimated covariance matrix of base forecast errors, optimally weighting forecasts based on their error correlations.

```python
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from scipy.linalg import inv

class HierarchicalReconciliation:
    """
    Reconciles hierarchical forecasts to ensure coherence.
    
    Implements bottom-up, top-down, and optimal reconciliation
    methods (OLS, WLS, MinT) for hierarchical time series.
    """
    def __init__(self, hierarchy_structure):
        """
        Args:
            hierarchy_structure: Dictionary defining hierarchy
                Example: {
                    'Total': ['RegionA', 'RegionB'],
                    'RegionA': ['StoreA1', 'StoreA2'],
                    'RegionB': ['StoreB1', 'StoreB2']
                }
        """
        self.hierarchy = hierarchy_structure
        self.summing_matrix = self._build_summing_matrix()
        self.W = None  # Weight matrix for reconciliation
        
    def _build_summing_matrix(self):
        """
        Construct summing matrix S that maps bottom-level to all levels.
        
        S has shape (m, n) where m is total series and n is bottom-level series.
        Each row shows how to aggregate bottom series for that level.
        """
        # Get all nodes in hierarchy
        all_nodes = self._get_all_nodes()
        bottom_nodes = self._get_bottom_nodes()
        
        n_bottom = len(bottom_nodes)
        n_total = len(all_nodes)
        
        S = np.zeros((n_total, n_bottom))
        
        for i, node in enumerate(all_nodes):
            if node in bottom_nodes:
                # Bottom-level series: identity mapping
                j = bottom_nodes.index(node)
                S[i, j] = 1.0
            else:
                # Aggregate series: sum of children
                children = self._get_all_descendants(node)
                for child in children:
                    if child in bottom_nodes:
                        j = bottom_nodes.index(child)
                        S[i, j] = 1.0
        
        return S
    
    def _get_all_nodes(self):
        """Get all nodes in hierarchy (breadth-first order)."""
        nodes = []
        queue = [list(self.hierarchy.keys())[0]]  # Start from root
        visited = set()
        
        while queue:
            node = queue.pop(0)
            if node in visited:
                continue
            visited.add(node)
            nodes.append(node)
            
            if node in self.hierarchy:
                queue.extend(self.hierarchy[node])
        
        return nodes
    
    def _get_bottom_nodes(self):
        """Get leaf nodes (no children)."""
        all_nodes = set(self._get_all_nodes())
        parent_nodes = set(self.hierarchy.keys())
        return sorted(list(all_nodes - parent_nodes))
    
    def _get_all_descendants(self, node):
        """Get all descendant nodes (recursive)."""
        if node not in self.hierarchy:
            return [node]
        descendants = []
        for child in self.hierarchy[node]:
            descendants.extend(self._get_all_descendants(child))
        return descendants
    
    def fit(self, base_forecast_errors, method='mint'):
        """
        Estimate weight matrix from historical forecast errors.
        
        Args:
            base_forecast_errors: DataFrame with columns for each series
                                 and rows for each historical period
            method: 'ols', 'wls', 'mint'
        """
        n_series = base_forecast_errors.shape[1]
        
        if method == 'ols':
            # Ordinary least squares: identity matrix
            self.W = np.eye(n_series)
            
        elif method == 'wls':
            # Weighted least squares: diagonal of variances
            variances = base_forecast_errors.var(axis=0).values
            self.W = np.diag(1 / (variances + 1e-8))
            
        elif method == 'mint':
            # Minimum trace: full covariance matrix
            # This optimally weights based on error correlations
            cov_matrix = base_forecast_errors.cov().values
            # Add small diagonal for numerical stability
            cov_matrix += np.eye(n_series) * 1e-8
            self.W = inv(cov_matrix)
            
        else:
            raise ValueError(f"Unknown method: {method}")
        
        # Precompute reconciliation matrix for efficiency
        # This is the matrix that transforms base forecasts to reconciled forecasts
        S = self.summing_matrix
        self.G = S @ inv(S.T @ self.W @ S) @ S.T @ self.W
        
        return self
    
    def reconcile(self, base_forecasts):
        """
        Reconcile base forecasts to ensure coherence.
        
        Args:
            base_forecasts: Array or Series of base forecasts for all levels
        
        Returns:
            Reconciled forecasts that satisfy coherence constraints
        """
        if self.W is None:
            raise ValueError("Must call fit() before reconcile()")
        
        y_hat = np.array(base_forecasts).flatten()
        
        # Apply reconciliation: y_tilde = G @ y_hat
        y_reconciled = self.G @ y_hat
        
        return y_reconciled
    
    def bottom_up(self, bottom_forecasts):
        """
        Bottom-up forecasting: aggregate from bottom level.
        Simple but ignores aggregate-level information.
        """
        b_hat = np.array(bottom_forecasts).flatten()
        y_hat = self.summing_matrix @ b_hat
        return y_hat
    
    def top_down(self, top_forecast, historical_proportions):
        """
        Top-down forecasting: distribute top level using proportions.
        
        Args:
            top_forecast: Scalar forecast for top level
            historical_proportions: Array of historical bottom-level proportions
        """
        # Distribute top forecast according to proportions
        bottom_forecasts = top_forecast * historical_proportions
        
        # Build full hierarchy using bottom-up
        return self.bottom_up(bottom_forecasts)
```

### Forecast Reconciliation in Practice

Implementing hierarchical forecasting in production requires careful consideration of computational costs and data quality. For large hierarchies with thousands of bottom-level series, computing the full covariance matrix for MinT reconciliation becomes prohibitively expensive. In such cases, we can use shrinkage estimators that combine the sample covariance with a structured prior, or we can use sparse covariance estimation that assumes most series have uncorrelated errors.

The choice of reconciliation method depends on the data characteristics. If bottom-level series are very noisy with small sample sizes, top-down or MinT reconciliation often performs better by borrowing strength from aggregate levels. If bottom-level series have strong individual trends that differ from the aggregate, bottom-up or light reconciliation that stays close to base forecasts works better. Cross-validation across the hierarchy can help select the optimal reconciliation approach.

## 8.3 Probabilistic Forecasting and Conformal Prediction

### Beyond Point Forecasts: Quantifying Uncertainty

Most forecasting applications require more than point predictions. Decision makers need to understand forecast uncertainty to manage risk appropriately. A retailer deciding on inventory levels needs to know not just the expected demand but also the range of plausible outcomes. A utility company planning capacity needs to understand the probability of extreme demand events. Probabilistic forecasting provides full predictive distributions or prediction intervals rather than single point estimates.

### Parametric Prediction Intervals

Traditional prediction intervals assume errors follow a parametric distribution, typically normal. For a forecast y_hat_t, a ninety-five percent prediction interval is y_hat_t plus or minus 1.96 times sigma_hat, where sigma_hat estimates the standard deviation of forecast errors. This approach is simple and interpretable but relies on the normality assumption, which often fails for real data. Time series forecast errors frequently exhibit heteroscedasticity (changing variance), skewness, or heavy tails.

### Quantile Regression

Quantile regression provides a flexible approach to constructing prediction intervals without distributional assumptions. Instead of modeling the conditional mean E[Y|X], we model conditional quantiles Q_tau[Y|X] for different probability levels tau. The tau-quantile is the value q such that P(Y less than or equal to q | X) equals tau. For example, the 0.05 quantile and 0.95 quantile form a ninety percent prediction interval.

We estimate quantile forecasts by minimizing the pinball loss: L_tau(y, q) equals tau times (y minus q) if y is greater than or equal to q, otherwise (1 minus tau) times (q minus y). This asymmetric loss function penalizes overprediction and underprediction differently depending on the quantile. For tau equals 0.95, large underpredictions (actual exceeds prediction) are penalized heavily, while large overpredictions are penalized lightly.

```python
import numpy as np
from sklearn.ensemble import GradientBoostingRegressor

class QuantileForecaster:
    """
    Probabilistic forecasting using quantile regression.
    
    Estimates multiple conditional quantiles to form
    prediction intervals without distributional assumptions.
    """
    def __init__(self, quantiles=[0.05, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 0.95], 
                 base_model='gbm'):
        """
        Args:
            quantiles: List of quantiles to estimate (0 to 1)
            base_model: 'gbm' for gradient boosting or 'linear' for quantile regression
        """
        self.quantiles = sorted(quantiles)
        self.base_model = base_model
        self.models = {}
        
    def fit(self, X, y):
        """Train a separate model for each quantile."""
        for q in self.quantiles:
            if self.base_model == 'gbm':
                # Gradient boosting with quantile loss
                model = GradientBoostingRegressor(
                    loss='quantile',
                    alpha=q,
                    n_estimators=100,
                    max_depth=3,
                    learning_rate=0.1
                )
            else:
                # Linear quantile regression
                from sklearn.linear_model import QuantileRegressor
                model = QuantileRegressor(quantile=q, alpha=0.1)
            
            model.fit(X, y)
            self.models[q] = model
        
        return self
    
    def predict(self, X, return_all_quantiles=False):
        """
        Generate probabilistic forecast.
        
        Args:
            X: Features for prediction
            return_all_quantiles: If True, return all quantiles.
                                 If False, return median (0.5 quantile)
        
        Returns:
            If return_all_quantiles=True: dict mapping quantile to predictions
            If return_all_quantiles=False: median predictions
        """
        predictions = {}
        for q in self.quantiles:
            predictions[q] = self.models[q].predict(X)
        
        if return_all_quantiles:
            return predictions
        else:
            # Return median as point forecast
            return predictions[0.5] if 0.5 in predictions else predictions[self.quantiles[len(self.quantiles)//2]]
    
    def predict_interval(self, X, confidence_level=0.90):
        """
        Generate prediction interval at specified confidence level.
        
        Args:
            X: Features for prediction
            confidence_level: Coverage probability (e.g., 0.90 for 90% interval)
        
        Returns:
            Tuple of (lower_bound, median, upper_bound) arrays
        """
        alpha = 1 - confidence_level
        lower_q = alpha / 2
        upper_q = 1 - alpha / 2
        
        # Find closest quantiles we have
        lower_quantile = min(self.quantiles, key=lambda x: abs(x - lower_q))
        upper_quantile = min(self.quantiles, key=lambda x: abs(x - upper_q))
        median_quantile = min(self.quantiles, key=lambda x: abs(x - 0.5))
        
        lower = self.models[lower_quantile].predict(X)
        median = self.models[median_quantile].predict(X)
        upper = self.models[upper_quantile].predict(X)
        
        return lower, median, upper
    
    def coverage_diagnostics(self, X, y, confidence_level=0.90):
        """
        Evaluate whether prediction intervals achieve nominal coverage.
        
        Computes the proportion of actual values falling within
        prediction intervals and checks if this matches confidence_level.
        """
        lower, median, upper = self.predict_interval(X, confidence_level)
        
        # Count how many actuals fall in the interval
        in_interval = (y >= lower) & (y <= upper)
        empirical_coverage = in_interval.mean()
        
        # Compute interval width
        mean_width = (upper - lower).mean()
        
        return {
            'nominal_coverage': confidence_level,
            'empirical_coverage': empirical_coverage,
            'coverage_error': empirical_coverage - confidence_level,
            'mean_interval_width': mean_width,
            'n_samples': len(y)
        }
```

### Conformal Prediction: Distribution-Free Intervals with Guaranteed Coverage

Conformal prediction offers a remarkable guarantee: prediction intervals with coverage guarantees that hold for any distribution, any model, and any data generating process. The key insight is to use past errors to calibrate future prediction intervals, making no assumptions about the form of the errors.

The method works as follows. We split data into a training set and calibration set. We train our forecasting model on the training set and compute forecast errors on the calibration set: e_i equals absolute value of y_i minus y_hat_i for i equals one to n_cal. We sort these errors in ascending order. To form a prediction interval with coverage one minus alpha, we use the quantity alpha times (n_cal plus one) divided by n_cal quantile of the calibration errors. For a new prediction y_hat_new, the conformal prediction interval is [y_hat_new minus q_alpha, y_hat_new plus q_alpha].

The theoretical guarantee states that if the calibration data and test data are exchangeable (a weaker condition than independence), then P(y_new in the interval) is greater than or equal to one minus alpha. This holds regardless of whether our model is correct, whether errors are normal, or whether observations are independent over time.

```python
import numpy as np

class ConformalPredictor:
    """
    Distribution-free prediction intervals with guaranteed coverage.
    
    Uses conformal prediction to calibrate any forecasting model,
    providing prediction intervals with valid coverage under
    exchangeability assumption.
    """
    def __init__(self, base_model):
        """
        Args:
            base_model: Any fitted forecasting model with predict() method
        """
        self.base_model = base_model
        self.calibration_scores = None
        
    def calibrate(self, X_cal, y_cal):
        """
        Compute nonconformity scores on calibration set.
        
        These scores measure how unusual each prediction error is.
        We use absolute residuals as the nonconformity measure.
        """
        # Get predictions on calibration set
        y_pred_cal = self.base_model.predict(X_cal)
        
        # Compute absolute residuals (nonconformity scores)
        self.calibration_scores = np.abs(y_cal - y_pred_cal)
        
        return self
    
    def predict_interval(self, X, confidence_level=0.90):
        """
        Generate conformal prediction interval.
        
        Returns interval with guaranteed coverage at confidence_level,
        assuming exchangeability between calibration and test data.
        """
        if self.calibration_scores is None:
            raise ValueError("Must call calibrate() before predict_interval()")
        
        # Get point predictions
        y_pred = self.base_model.predict(X)
        
        # Compute quantile of calibration scores
        alpha = 1 - confidence_level
        n_cal = len(self.calibration_scores)
        
        # Adjusted quantile for finite-sample correction
        # This ensures coverage of at least (1-alpha)
        q_level = np.ceil((n_cal + 1) * (1 - alpha)) / n_cal
        q_level = min(q_level, 1.0)  # Cap at 1.0
        
        # Get the quantile of calibration scores
        interval_width = np.quantile(self.calibration_scores, q_level)
        
        # Form symmetric interval around prediction
        lower = y_pred - interval_width
        upper = y_pred + interval_width
        
        return lower, y_pred, upper
    
    def evaluate_coverage(self, X_test, y_test, confidence_level=0.90):
        """
        Verify that empirical coverage matches theoretical guarantee.
        
        Should see empirical coverage >= confidence_level
        (may exceed due to discreteness and finite sample correction).
        """
        lower, pred, upper = self.predict_interval(X_test, confidence_level)
        
        # Check coverage
        in_interval = (y_test >= lower) & (y_test <= upper)
        empirical_coverage = in_interval.mean()
        
        # Compute interval width
        mean_width = (upper - lower).mean()
        
        return {
            'target_coverage': confidence_level,
            'empirical_coverage': empirical_coverage,
            'mean_width': mean_width,
            'n_test': len(y_test),
            'coverage_achieved': empirical_coverage >= confidence_level
        }


class AdaptiveConformalPredictor(ConformalPredictor):
    """
    Adaptive conformal prediction for time series.
    
    Extends basic conformal prediction to handle changing error
    distributions over time by using weighted calibration scores.
    """
    def __init__(self, base_model, window_size=100, decay_rate=0.95):
        """
        Args:
            base_model: Fitted forecasting model
            window_size: Size of sliding window for calibration
            decay_rate: Exponential decay for weighting recent scores
        """
        super().__init__(base_model)
        self.window_size = window_size
        self.decay_rate = decay_rate
        self.online_scores = []
        
    def update(self, X_new, y_new):
        """
        Update calibration scores with new observation.
        
        Maintains sliding window of recent scores with
        exponential weighting favoring recent data.
        """
        # Get prediction for new observation
        y_pred_new = self.base_model.predict(X_new.reshape(1, -1))[0]
        
        # Compute nonconformity score
        score = abs(y_new - y_pred_new)
        
        # Add to online scores
        self.online_scores.append(score)
        
        # Maintain window
        if len(self.online_scores) > self.window_size:
            self.online_scores.pop(0)
    
    def predict_interval_adaptive(self, X, confidence_level=0.90):
        """
        Generate prediction interval using weighted recent scores.
        
        Adapts to changing error distributions by giving more
        weight to recent calibration scores.
        """
        if len(self.online_scores) < 10:
            raise ValueError("Need at least 10 online scores for adaptive intervals")
        
        # Get point prediction
        y_pred = self.base_model.predict(X)
        
        # Compute exponentially weighted quantile
        scores = np.array(self.online_scores)
        n = len(scores)
        
        # Create exponential weights (recent scores get higher weight)
        weights = np.array([self.decay_rate ** (n - 1 - i) for i in range(n)])
        weights = weights / weights.sum()  # Normalize
        
        # Compute weighted quantile
        alpha = 1 - confidence_level
        sorted_indices = np.argsort(scores)
        sorted_scores = scores[sorted_indices]
        sorted_weights = weights[sorted_indices]
        cumsum_weights = np.cumsum(sorted_weights)
        
        # Find quantile
        q_idx = np.searchsorted(cumsum_weights, 1 - alpha)
        q_idx = min(q_idx, n - 1)
        interval_width = sorted_scores[q_idx]
        
        lower = y_pred - interval_width
        upper = y_pred + interval_width
        
        return lower, y_pred, upper
```

### Time Series Complications for Conformal Prediction

Applying conformal prediction to time series requires careful handling of temporal dependencies. The theoretical coverage guarantee assumes exchangeability, which is violated when observations are serially correlated. Several approaches address this issue. The block conformal approach treats contiguous blocks of time as exchangeable units rather than individual observations. The residual conformal approach applies conformal prediction to the residuals after removing autocorrelation structure. The adaptive conformal approach uses a sliding window of recent calibration data and exponential weighting to handle distribution drift.

## 8.4 Causal Inference in Time Series

### Correlation vs. Causation: Why It Matters for Forecasting

Forecasting models typically focus on prediction, not causation. We build models that correlate past patterns with future outcomes without necessarily understanding causal mechanisms. This works well when the data generating process remains stable, but fails catastrophically when the system changes. Consider forecasting ice cream sales using temperature as a predictor. The correlation is strong, but if we causally intervene by artificially heating the weather station, ice cream sales will not change because temperature does not cause sales; both are caused by underlying climate patterns.

Understanding causality becomes critical when we want to forecast the impact of interventions or policy changes. A retailer wants to know: if we reduce price by ten percent, how will sales change? A central bank wants to know: if we raise interest rates, how will inflation respond? These are causal questions that correlation-based models cannot answer reliably.

### The Potential Outcomes Framework

The fundamental problem of causal inference is that we can never observe both potential outcomes for the same unit. If we treat a customer with a discount, we observe their purchase behavior under treatment but not their counterfactual behavior without treatment. The causal effect is defined as Y_i(1) minus Y_i(0), where Y_i(1) is the outcome under treatment and Y_i(0) is the outcome under control. We can never observe both for the same individual i.

In time series, we often have a single treated unit at a single treatment time. For example, a new policy is implemented nationwide on a specific date. We observe the outcome trajectory before treatment and after treatment, but we do not observe the counterfactual trajectory that would have occurred without treatment. The key challenge is constructing a credible counterfactual.

### Synthetic Control Method

The synthetic control method constructs a counterfactual by finding a weighted combination of control units that closely matches the treated unit's pre-treatment characteristics. The intuition is that if we can create a synthetic version of the treated unit using untreated units, and this synthetic control tracked the treated unit closely before treatment, then differences after treatment plausibly represent causal effects.

Formally, suppose we have J plus one units with unit one treated at time T_0. We observe outcomes Y_it for unit i at time t, with t equals one to T_0 minus one being pre-treatment and t equals T_0 to T being post-treatment. We find weights w equals (w_2, ..., w_J+1) that minimize the pre-treatment fit: minimize the sum from t equals one to T_0 minus one of the quantity Y_1t minus the sum from j equals two to J plus one of w_j times Y_jt all squared, subject to w_j is greater than or equal to zero and the sum of w_j equals one.

The causal effect estimate at time t is greater than T_0 is hat_tau_1t equals Y_1t minus the sum from j equals two to J plus one of hat_w_j times Y_jt, where hat_w_j are the estimated optimal weights.

```python
import numpy as np
from scipy.optimize import minimize
import pandas as pd

class SyntheticControl:
    """
    Causal inference using synthetic control method.
    
    Constructs counterfactual trajectory from weighted combination
    of control units to estimate causal effect of intervention.
    """
    def __init__(self):
        self.weights = None
        self.treated_unit = None
        self.control_units = None
        
    def fit(self, treated_data, control_data, covariates=None):
        """
        Find optimal weights for synthetic control.
        
        Args:
            treated_data: Series of pre-treatment outcomes for treated unit
            control_data: DataFrame with columns for each control unit,
                         rows for each pre-treatment time period
            covariates: Optional DataFrame of additional covariates to match
        
        Returns:
            Optimal weights for control units
        """
        self.treated_unit = treated_data.values
        self.control_units = control_data.values
        
        n_controls = self.control_units.shape[1]
        
        # Objective: minimize pre-treatment prediction error
        def objective(w):
            """Squared error between treated and synthetic control."""
            synthetic = self.control_units @ w
            return np.sum((self.treated_unit - synthetic) ** 2)
        
        # Constraints: weights sum to 1 and are non-negative
        constraints = {
            'type': 'eq',
            'fun': lambda w: np.sum(w) - 1.0
        }
        
        bounds = [(0, 1) for _ in range(n_controls)]
        
        # Initial guess: equal weights
        w0 = np.ones(n_controls) / n_controls
        
        # Optimize
        result = minimize(
            objective,
            w0,
            method='SLSQP',
            bounds=bounds,
            constraints=constraints,
            options={'ftol': 1e-9, 'maxiter': 1000}
        )
        
        if not result.success:
            print(f"Warning: Optimization did not converge: {result.message}")
        
        self.weights = result.x
        
        return self.weights
    
    def predict_counterfactual(self, control_data_full):
        """
        Generate counterfactual trajectory for full time period.
        
        Args:
            control_data_full: DataFrame of control unit data including
                              both pre-treatment and post-treatment periods
        
        Returns:
            Synthetic control trajectory
        """
        if self.weights is None:
            raise ValueError("Must call fit() before predict_counterfactual()")
        
        synthetic = control_data_full.values @ self.weights
        
        return pd.Series(synthetic, index=control_data_full.index)
    
    def estimate_effects(self, treated_full, control_data_full, treatment_time):
        """
        Estimate causal effects post-treatment.
        
        Args:
            treated_full: Full treated unit trajectory (pre + post)
            control_data_full: Full control unit data
            treatment_time: Time index when treatment occurred
        
        Returns:
            DataFrame with actual, counterfactual, and causal effect
        """
        if self.weights is None:
            raise ValueError("Must call fit() before estimate_effects()")
        
        # Generate synthetic control for full period
        synthetic = self.predict_counterfactual(control_data_full)
        
        # Compute treatment effects
        effects = treated_full - synthetic
        
        results = pd.DataFrame({
            'treated': treated_full,
            'synthetic_control': synthetic,
            'effect': effects,
            'post_treatment': treated_full.index >= treatment_time
        })
        
        return results
    
    def placebo_test(self, control_data, treatment_time, n_placebos='all'):
        """
        Conduct placebo tests by applying synthetic control to control units.
        
        Assigns treatment to control units where no treatment occurred
        to assess whether estimated effects are unusually large.
        """
        if n_placebos == 'all':
            n_placebos = control_data.shape[1]
        
        n_pre = (control_data.index < treatment_time).sum()
        
        placebo_effects = []
        
        for i in range(min(n_placebos, control_data.shape[1])):
            # Treat control unit i as if it were treated
            pseudo_treated = control_data.iloc[:n_pre, i]
            pseudo_controls = control_data.iloc[:n_pre, :].drop(control_data.columns[i], axis=1)
            
            # Fit synthetic control
            try:
                self.fit(pseudo_treated, pseudo_controls)
                
                # Compute post-treatment "effects" (should be near zero)
                pseudo_treated_full = control_data.iloc[:, i]
                pseudo_controls_full = control_data.drop(control_data.columns[i], axis=1)
                synthetic = self.predict_counterfactual(pseudo_controls_full)
                
                placebo_effect = pseudo_treated_full - synthetic
                placebo_effects.append(placebo_effect)
            except:
                # Skip if optimization fails
                continue
        
        return pd.DataFrame(placebo_effects).T
    
    def inference(self, treated_full, control_data_full, treatment_time, 
                 n_permutations=100):
        """
        Conduct permutation-based inference.
        
        Compares actual treatment effect to distribution of placebo effects
        to assess statistical significance.
        """
        # Get actual treatment effect
        actual_effects = self.estimate_effects(
            treated_full, 
            control_data_full, 
            treatment_time
        )
        actual_post_effect = actual_effects[actual_effects['post_treatment']]['effect'].mean()
        
        # Conduct placebo tests
        placebo_effects = self.placebo_test(
            control_data_full,
            treatment_time,
            n_placebos=n_permutations
        )
        
        # Get post-treatment placebo effects
        post_placebo = placebo_effects.loc[treatment_time:]
        avg_placebo_effects = post_placebo.mean(axis=0)
        
        # Compute p-value: proportion of placebos as extreme as actual
        p_value = (np.abs(avg_placebo_effects) >= np.abs(actual_post_effect)).mean()
        
        return {
            'actual_effect': actual_post_effect,
            'p_value': p_value,
            'placebo_distribution': avg_placebo_effects,
            'significant': p_value < 0.05
        }
```

### Difference-in-Differences

When synthetic control is not feasible (perhaps we lack good control units), difference-in-differences provides an alternative approach. The method compares the change in outcomes for treated units before and after treatment to the change in outcomes for control units over the same period. The key assumption is parallel trends: in the absence of treatment, treated and control units would have followed parallel trajectories.

The estimand is: hat_tau equals (y_treated_post minus y_treated_pre) minus (y_control_post minus y_control_pre). This double difference removes time-invariant differences between treated and control units (first difference) and removes common time trends (second difference).

For time series implementation, we estimate using regression: Y_it equals alpha plus beta times Treated_i plus gamma times Post_t plus tau times (Treated_i times Post_t) plus epsilon_it, where tau is our causal effect estimate. The interaction term captures the differential change for treated units after treatment.

### Granger Causality: Predictive Causality for Stationary Processes

Granger causality offers a different notion of causality focused on prediction. We say X Granger-causes Y if past values of X contain information that helps predict Y beyond what is contained in past values of Y alone. This does not prove true causality but suggests a predictive relationship that may reflect underlying causal mechanisms.

The test compares two models. The restricted model uses only lags of Y: Y_t equals alpha_0 plus the sum from i equals one to p of alpha_i times Y_t minus i plus epsilon_t. The unrestricted model includes lags of X: Y_t equals beta_0 plus the sum from i equals one to p of beta_i times Y_t minus i plus the sum from j equals one to q of gamma_j times X_t minus j plus nu_t. We test the null hypothesis that all gamma_j equal zero using an F-test. Rejecting the null suggests X Granger-causes Y.

---

# Section 9: Complete Real-World Case Study

## Overview: Airline Passenger Forecasting

This case study demonstrates a complete time series forecasting workflow using the classic airline passenger dataset. We will progress through all stages from initial exploration to production deployment, implementing techniques from all previous sections. The dataset contains monthly totals of international airline passengers from 1949 to 1960, exhibiting clear trend, seasonality, and increasing variance over time.

The business context is crucial for framing our analysis. An airline needs accurate passenger forecasts for capacity planning, crew scheduling, pricing optimization, and financial projections. Underforecasting leads to capacity constraints, customer dissatisfaction, and lost revenue. Overforecasting leads to empty seats, excessive crew costs, and wasted capital expenditure on unnecessary aircraft. The asymmetric costs of errors may require specialized loss functions beyond simple mean squared error.
