### Statistical Tests for Stationarity

```python
def test_stationarity(timeseries, window=12):
    """
    Perform multiple tests for stationarity and visualize.
    
    Tests:
    1. Augmented Dickey-Fuller (ADF): null hypothesis is non-stationary
    2. KPSS: null hypothesis is stationary
    3. Rolling statistics visualization
    """
    print("="*60)
    print("STATIONARITY TESTS")
    print("="*60)
    
    # Rolling statistics
    rolling_mean = timeseries.rolling(window=window).mean()
    rolling_std = timeseries.rolling(window=window).std()
    
    # Plot
    fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(12, 6))
    ax.plot(timeseries.index, timeseries, label='Original', linewidth=2)
    ax.plot(rolling_mean.index, rolling_mean, label=f'{window}-Month Rolling Mean', 
            linewidth=2, linestyle='--')
    ax.plot(rolling_std.index, rolling_std, label=f'{window}-Month Rolling Std', 
            linewidth=2, linestyle=':')
    ax.legend()
    ax.set_title('Rolling Mean and Standard Deviation')
    ax.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
    plt.show()
    
    # ADF Test
    print("\n1. Augmented Dickey-Fuller Test:")
    print("   Null Hypothesis: Series is non-stationary (has unit root)")
    adf_result = adfuller(timeseries.dropna(), autolag='AIC')
    print(f"   ADF Statistic: {adf_result[0]:.4f}")
    print(f"   p-value: {adf_result[1]:.4f}")
    print(f"   Critical Values:")
    for key, value in adf_result[4].items():
        print(f"      {key}: {value:.4f}")
    
    if adf_result[1] < 0.05:
        print("   ✓ Reject null hypothesis: Series appears STATIONARY")
    else:
        print("   ✗ Fail to reject null hypothesis: Series appears NON-STATIONARY")
    
    # KPSS Test
    print("\n2. KPSS Test:")
    print("   Null Hypothesis: Series is stationary")
    kpss_result = kpss(timeseries.dropna(), regression='ct')
    print(f"   KPSS Statistic: {kpss_result[0]:.4f}")
    print(f"   p-value: {kpss_result[1]:.4f}")
    print(f"   Critical Values:")
    for key, value in kpss_result[3].items():
        print(f"      {key}: {value:.4f}")
    
    if kpss_result[1] < 0.05:
        print("   ✗ Reject null hypothesis: Series appears NON-STATIONARY")
    else:
        print("   ✓ Fail to reject null hypothesis: Series appears STATIONARY")
    
    print("\n" + "="*60)

# Test original series
test_stationarity(df['Passengers'])
```

The tests reveal strong non-stationarity. The ADF test fails to reject the null hypothesis of a unit root with a p-value above 0.05, and the KPSS test rejects stationarity with p-value below 0.05. The rolling mean clearly increases over time, and the rolling standard deviation also grows, indicating both trend and heteroscedasticity.

### Decomposition

```python
# Classical decomposition (additive and multiplicative)
decomposition_add = seasonal_decompose(df['Passengers'], model='additive', period=12)
decomposition_mult = seasonal_decompose(df['Passengers'], model='multiplicative', period=12)

# Visualize
fig, axes = plt.subplots(4, 2, figsize=(15, 12))
fig.suptitle('Time Series Decomposition: Additive vs Multiplicative', 
             fontsize=16, fontweight='bold')

# Additive
decomposition_add.observed.plot(ax=axes[0, 0], title='Observed (Additive)')
decomposition_add.trend.plot(ax=axes[1, 0], title='Trend')
decomposition_add.seasonal.plot(ax=axes[2, 0], title='Seasonal')
decomposition_add.resid.plot(ax=axes[3, 0], title='Residual')

# Multiplicative
decomposition_mult.observed.plot(ax=axes[0, 1], title='Observed (Multiplicative)')
decomposition_mult.trend.plot(ax=axes[1, 1], title='Trend')
decomposition_mult.seasonal.plot(ax=axes[2, 1], title='Seasonal')
decomposition_mult.resid.plot(ax=axes[3, 1], title='Residual')

for ax in axes.flat:
    ax.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
    ax.set_xlabel('')

plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('decomposition.png', dpi=300, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

# Analyze residuals
print("\nResidual Analysis:")
print(f"Additive model - Residual std: {decomposition_add.resid.std():.2f}")
print(f"Multiplicative model - Residual std: {decomposition_mult.resid.std():.2f}")

# Test if residuals are white noise
from statsmodels.stats.diagnostic import acorr_ljungbox
lb_test_add = acorr_ljungbox(decomposition_add.resid.dropna(), lags=[12, 24])
print(f"\nLjung-Box test on additive residuals:")
print(f"  p-value (lag 12): {lb_test_add['lb_pvalue'].iloc[0]:.4f}")
print(f"  p-value (lag 24): {lb_test_add['lb_pvalue'].iloc[1]:.4f}")
```

The decomposition reveals a strong upward trend throughout the period, clear monthly seasonality with peaks in summer months (June-August), and residuals that become larger in magnitude over time. The multiplicative decomposition produces more stable residuals, suggesting that the seasonal component grows proportionally with the trend rather than remaining additive.

### Key Findings from EDA

Our exploratory analysis reveals several critical patterns. The series exhibits a strong positive trend with passengers increasing from around 100 thousand to over 600 thousand during the twelve-year period. There is pronounced monthly seasonality with summer months (June through August) showing consistently higher passenger counts. The variance increases over time, indicating heteroscedasticity that violates assumptions of many statistical models. The series is clearly non-stationary, requiring differencing or transformation before applying models that assume stationarity. The ACF shows slow decay characteristic of non-stationary series, while seasonal lags at twelve, twenty-four, and thirty-six months show high autocorrelation. Finally, the multiplicative decomposition better captures the changing amplitude of seasonality over time.

## Phase 2: Data Preprocessing and Feature Engineering

### Train-Test Split with Temporal Considerations

```python
# Split data maintaining temporal order
# Use last 12 months as test set
train_size = len(df) - 12
train = df.iloc[:train_size].copy()
test = df.iloc[train_size:].copy()

print(f"Training period: {train.index[0]} to {train.index[-1]}")
print(f"Training samples: {len(train)}")
print(f"\nTest period: {test.index[0]} to {test.index[-1]}")
print(f"Test samples: {len(test)}")

# Further split train into train and validation for hyperparameter tuning
# Use last 12 months of training data as validation
val_size = 12
train_fit = train.iloc[:-val_size].copy()
val = train.iloc[-val_size:].copy()

print(f"\nFinal training samples: {len(train_fit)}")
print(f"Validation samples: {len(val)}")
```

### Transformations to Address Non-Stationarity

```python
import warnings
warnings.filterwarnings('ignore')

# Apply log transformation to stabilize variance
df['Passengers_log'] = np.log(df['Passengers'])
train['Passengers_log'] = np.log(train['Passengers'])
test['Passengers_log'] = np.log(test['Passengers'])
train_fit['Passengers_log'] = np.log(train_fit['Passengers'])
val['Passengers_log'] = np.log(val['Passengers'])

# First difference to remove trend
df['Passengers_diff1'] = df['Passengers_log'].diff()
train['Passengers_diff1'] = train['Passengers_log'].diff()

# Seasonal difference (lag 12) to remove seasonality
df['Passengers_diff12'] = df['Passengers_log'].diff(12)
train['Passengers_diff12'] = train['Passengers_log'].diff(12)

# Combined: first difference of seasonal difference
df['Passengers_diff1_12'] = df['Passengers_log'].diff(12).diff()
train['Passengers_diff1_12'] = train['Passengers_log'].diff(12).diff()

# Test stationarity of transformations
print("Testing transformed series for stationarity:\n")

transformations = {
    'Log': df['Passengers_log'],
    'Log + First Diff': df['Passengers_diff1'].dropna(),
    'Log + Seasonal Diff': df['Passengers_diff12'].dropna(),
    'Log + First + Seasonal Diff': df['Passengers_diff1_12'].dropna()
}

results = []
for name, series in transformations.items():
    adf_result = adfuller(series.dropna(), autolag='AIC')
    results.append({
        'Transformation': name,
        'ADF Statistic': adf_result[0],
        'p-value': adf_result[1],
        'Stationary?': 'Yes' if adf_result[1] < 0.05 else 'No'
    })

results_df = pd.DataFrame(results)
print(results_df.to_string(index=False))
```

The combined log transformation with first and seasonal differencing achieves stationarity, as confirmed by the ADF test p-value falling below 0.05. This transformation will be used for models requiring stationary input.

### Feature Engineering for Machine Learning Models

```python
def create_lag_features(df, target_col, lags, horizon=1):
    """
    Create lagged features for time series forecasting.
    
    Args:
        df: DataFrame with datetime index
        target_col: Name of target variable
        lags: List of lag values to create
        horizon: Forecast horizon (shifts target forward)
    
    Returns:
        DataFrame with lag features and shifted target
    """
    result = df.copy()
    
    # Create lagged features
    for lag in lags:
        result[f'lag_{lag}'] = result[target_col].shift(lag)
    
    # Shift target forward for multi-step ahead prediction
    if horizon > 1:
        result[f'{target_col}_target'] = result[target_col].shift(-horizon + 1)
    else:
        result[f'{target_col}_target'] = result[target_col]
    
    return result

def create_time_features(df):
    """
    Create calendar-based features from datetime index.
    """
    result = df.copy()
    
    result['month'] = result.index.month
    result['quarter'] = result.index.quarter
    result['year'] = result.index.year
    
    # Cyclical encoding for month (captures circular nature)
    result['month_sin'] = np.sin(2 * np.pi * result['month'] / 12)
    result['month_cos'] = np.cos(2 * np.pi * result['month'] / 12)
    
    return result

def create_rolling_features(df, target_col, windows):
    """
    Create rolling window statistics.
    """
    result = df.copy()
    
    for window in windows:
        result[f'rolling_mean_{window}'] = result[target_col].shift(1).rolling(window).mean()
        result[f'rolling_std_{window}'] = result[target_col].shift(1).rolling(window).std()
        result[f'rolling_min_{window}'] = result[target_col].shift(1).rolling(window).min()
        result[f'rolling_max_{window}'] = result[target_col].shift(1).rolling(window).max()
    
    return result

# Create comprehensive feature set
lags = [1, 2, 3, 6, 12]  # Recent lags and seasonal lag
windows = [3, 6, 12]  # Short, medium, long-term windows

train_ml = create_lag_features(train.copy(), 'Passengers', lags)
train_ml = create_time_features(train_ml)
train_ml = create_rolling_features(train_ml, 'Passengers', windows)

# Remove rows with NaN from feature creation
train_ml = train_ml.dropna()

print(f"Machine learning features created: {train_ml.shape[1]} columns")
print(f"\nFeature names:")
print(train_ml.columns.tolist())
print(f"\nTraining samples after feature engineering: {len(train_ml)}")
```

## Phase 3: Baseline Models

### Naive Forecasts

```python
from sklearn.metrics import mean_absolute_error, mean_squared_error, mean_absolute_percentage_error

def evaluate_forecast(actual, predicted, model_name):
    """
    Calculate comprehensive evaluation metrics.
    """
    mae = mean_absolute_error(actual, predicted)
    rmse = np.sqrt(mean_squared_error(actual, predicted))
    mape = mean_absolute_percentage_error(actual, predicted) * 100
    
    # Additional metrics
    bias = np.mean(predicted - actual)
    
    return {
        'Model': model_name,
        'MAE': mae,
        'RMSE': rmse,
        'MAPE': mape,
        'Bias': bias
    }

# 1. Naive forecast: last observed value
naive_pred = np.repeat(train['Passengers'].iloc[-1], len(test))
naive_results = evaluate_forecast(test['Passengers'], naive_pred, 'Naive')

# 2. Seasonal naive: same month last year
seasonal_naive_pred = train['Passengers'].iloc[-12:].values
seasonal_naive_results = evaluate_forecast(test['Passengers'], seasonal_naive_pred, 'Seasonal Naive')

# 3. Average of last year
avg_last_year = train['Passengers'].iloc[-12:].mean()
avg_pred = np.repeat(avg_last_year, len(test))
avg_results = evaluate_forecast(test['Passengers'], avg_pred, 'Average Last Year')

# 4. Drift method: extends linear trend from first to last observation
slope = (train['Passengers'].iloc[-1] - train['Passengers'].iloc[0]) / (len(train) - 1)
drift_pred = train['Passengers'].iloc[-1] + slope * np.arange(1, len(test) + 1)
drift_results = evaluate_forecast(test['Passengers'], drift_pred, 'Drift')

# Create baseline results table
baseline_results = pd.DataFrame([naive_results, seasonal_naive_results, 
                                 avg_results, drift_results])
print("\nBaseline Model Performance:")
print(baseline_results.to_string(index=False))
```

The seasonal naive method typically performs best among simple baselines because it captures the strong seasonal pattern. This establishes our minimum performance threshold: any sophisticated model must beat seasonal naive to justify its complexity.

## Phase 4: Statistical Models (ARIMA Family)

### ARIMA Model Selection and Fitting

```python
from statsmodels.tsa.arima.model import ARIMA
from statsmodels.graphics.tsaplots import plot_predict
import itertools

# Grid search for ARIMA parameters
def arima_grid_search(train_data, p_range, d_range, q_range, seasonal=False):
    """
    Exhaustive search over ARIMA parameter space.
    Uses AIC for model selection.
    """
    best_aic = np.inf
    best_params = None
    best_seasonal_params = None
    
    # Generate all combinations
    if seasonal:
        param_combinations = itertools.product(p_range, d_range, q_range,
                                                p_range, d_range, q_range, [12])
    else:
        param_combinations = itertools.product(p_range, d_range, q_range)
    
    results_list = []
    
    for params in param_combinations:
        try:
            if seasonal:
                p, d, q, P, D, Q, m = params
                model = ARIMA(train_data, 
                             order=(p, d, q),
                             seasonal_order=(P, D, Q, m))
                params_str = f"ARIMA{(p,d,q)}x{(P,D,Q,m)}"
            else:
                p, d, q = params
                model = ARIMA(train_data, order=(p, d, q))
                params_str = f"ARIMA{(p,d,q)}"
            
            fitted = model.fit()
            aic = fitted.aic
            
            results_list.append({
                'params': params_str,
                'AIC': aic,
                'BIC': fitted.bic
            })
            
            if aic < best_aic:
                best_aic = aic
                best_params = params if not seasonal else params[:3]
                if seasonal:
                    best_seasonal_params = params[3:]
                    
        except Exception as e:
            continue
    
    results_df = pd.DataFrame(results_list).sort_values('AIC')
    
    return results_df, best_params, best_seasonal_params

# Search for best SARIMA model
print("Searching for optimal SARIMA parameters...")
print("This may take several minutes...\n")

# Narrow search space for computational efficiency
p_range = range(0, 3)
d_range = range(0, 2)
q_range = range(0, 3)

sarima_results, best_params, best_seasonal = arima_grid_search(
    train['Passengers'],
    p_range, d_range, q_range,
    seasonal=True
)

print("Top 10 models by AIC:")
print(sarima_results.head(10).to_string(index=False))

# Fit best model
p, d, q = best_params
P, D, Q, m = best_seasonal

best_sarima = ARIMA(train['Passengers'],
                    order=(p, d, q),
                    seasonal_order=(P, D, Q, m))
best_sarima_fit = best_sarima.fit()

print(f"\nBest SARIMA model: ARIMA{(p,d,q)}x{(P,D,Q,m)}")
print(best_sarima_fit.summary())
```

### Model Diagnostics

```python
# Comprehensive residual diagnostics
fig = best_sarima_fit.plot_diagnostics(figsize=(15, 10))
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('sarima_diagnostics.png', dpi=300, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

# Ljung-Box test for residual autocorrelation
lb_test = acorr_ljungbox(best_sarima_fit.resid, lags=[12, 24, 36])
print("\nLjung-Box Test (testing for remaining autocorrelation):")
print(lb_test)

if (lb_test['lb_pvalue'] > 0.05).all():
    print("\n✓ Residuals appear to be white noise (good fit)")
else:
    print("\n⚠ Some autocorrelation remains in residuals")
```

## Phase 5: Machine Learning Models

### Random Forest

```python
from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestRegressor
from sklearn.model_selection import TimeSeriesSplit, GridSearchCV

# Prepare features and target
feature_cols = [col for col in train_ml.columns if col not in ['Passengers', 'Passengers_target']]
X_train = train_ml[feature_cols]
y_train = train_ml['Passengers']

# Prepare test features (need to engineer same features)
test_ml = create_lag_features(test.copy(), 'Passengers', lags)
test_ml = create_time_features(test_ml)
test_ml = create_rolling_features(test_ml, 'Passengers', windows)

# For test features, we need to fill lags from training data
# This is slightly complex for a real forecast but demonstrates the concept
X_test = test_ml[feature_cols]

# Handle NaN in test features by forward filling from train
# In production, you'd handle this more carefully
for col in feature_cols:
    if X_test[col].isna().any():
        # Use last known value from training
        last_known = train_ml[col].iloc[-1]
        X_test[col].fillna(last_known, inplace=True)

# Random Forest with time series cross-validation
rf_params = {
    'n_estimators': [50, 100, 200],
    'max_depth': [5, 10, None],
    'min_samples_split': [2, 5, 10],
    'min_samples_leaf': [1, 2, 4]
}

tscv = TimeSeriesSplit(n_splits=3)

rf_model = RandomForestRegressor(random_state=42)
rf_grid = GridSearchCV(rf_model, rf_params, cv=tscv, 
                       scoring='neg_mean_absolute_error',
                       n_jobs=-1, verbose=1)

print("Training Random Forest with cross-validation...")
rf_grid.fit(X_train, y_train)

print(f"\nBest parameters: {rf_grid.best_params_}")
print(f"Best CV MAE: {-rf_grid.best_score_:.2f}")

# Forecast
rf_forecast = rf_grid.best_estimator_.predict(X_test)
rf_results = evaluate_forecast(test['Passengers'], rf_forecast, 'Random Forest')

# Feature importance
feature_importance = pd.DataFrame({
    'feature': feature_cols,
    'importance': rf_grid.best_estimator_.feature_importances_
}).sort_values('importance', ascending=False)

print("\nTop 10 Most Important Features:")
print(feature_importance.head(10).to_string(index=False))
```

## Phase 6: Ensemble Methods

### Simple Ensemble

```python
# Combine SARIMA, Random Forest, and XGBoost

# Equal weight ensemble
ensemble_simple = (sarima_forecast + rf_forecast + xgb_forecast) / 3
ensemble_simple_results = evaluate_forecast(test['Passengers'], ensemble_simple, 
                                            'Ensemble (Equal Weight)')

# Weighted by validation performance
# In a real scenario, you'd use a separate validation set
# Here we'll use inverse MAE as weights
weights = np.array([1/sarima_results['MAE'], 
                    1/rf_results['MAE'],
                    1/xgb_results['MAE']])
weights = weights / weights.sum()

ensemble_weighted = (weights[0] * sarima_forecast + 
                     weights[1] * rf_forecast +
                     weights[2] * xgb_forecast)
ensemble_weighted_results = evaluate_forecast(test['Passengers'], ensemble_weighted,
                                              'Ensemble (Weighted)')

print(f"\nEnsemble weights: SARIMA={weights[0]:.3f}, RF={weights[1]:.3f}, XGB={weights[2]:.3f}")
```

### Stacking Ensemble

```python
# Create stacking ensemble using cross-validation

# Generate out-of-fold predictions on training data
from sklearn.model_selection import cross_val_predict

# This is simplified - in practice you'd need more careful temporal handling
oof_sarima = np.zeros(len(train_ml))
oof_rf = cross_val_predict(rf_grid.best_estimator_, X_train, y_train, 
                           cv=tscv, n_jobs=-1)
oof_xgb = cross_val_predict(xgb_grid.best_estimator_, X_train, y_train,
                            cv=tscv, n_jobs=-1)

# For SARIMA, we'd need rolling forecasts - simplified here
# In production, implement proper time series cross-validation

# Stack predictions as features for meta-model
X_stack_train = np.column_stack([oof_rf, oof_xgb])
X_stack_test = np.column_stack([rf_forecast, xgb_forecast])

# Train meta-model (Ridge regression for stability)
from sklearn.linear_model import Ridge

meta_model = Ridge(alpha=1.0)
meta_model.fit(X_stack_train, y_train)

# Final predictions
stack_forecast = meta_model.predict(X_stack_test)
stack_results = evaluate_forecast(test['Passengers'], stack_forecast,
                                  'Stacking Ensemble')

print("\nMeta-model coefficients:")
print(f"Random Forest: {meta_model.coef_[0]:.3f}")
print(f"XGBoost: {meta_model.coef_[1]:.3f}")
```

## Phase 7: Probabilistic Forecasting

### Quantile Regression Forests

```python
from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestRegressor

class QuantileRandomForest:
    """
    Random Forest that can predict quantiles for prediction intervals.
    """
    def __init__(self, **kwargs):
        self.model = RandomForestRegressor(**kwargs)
        
    def fit(self, X, y):
        self.model.fit(X, y)
        return self
    
    def predict_quantiles(self, X, quantiles=[0.05, 0.5, 0.95]):
        """Predict multiple quantiles using tree predictions."""
        # Get predictions from all trees
        tree_predictions = np.array([tree.predict(X) for tree in self.model.estimators_])
        
        # Compute quantiles across trees
        quantile_predictions = {}
        for q in quantiles:
            quantile_predictions[q] = np.percentile(tree_predictions, q * 100, axis=0)
        
        return quantile_predictions

# Train quantile forest
qrf = QuantileRandomForest(n_estimators=200, max_depth=10, 
                           min_samples_leaf=2, random_state=42)
qrf.fit(X_train, y_train)

# Get prediction intervals
quantiles_pred = qrf.predict_quantiles(X_test, quantiles=[0.05, 0.5, 0.95])

# Visualize probabilistic forecast
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(15, 6))

ax.plot(test.index, test['Passengers'], label='Actual', linewidth=2, color='black')
ax.plot(test.index, quantiles_pred[0.5], label='Median Forecast', 
        linewidth=2, color='blue', linestyle='--')

ax.fill_between(test.index,
                quantiles_pred[0.05],
                quantiles_pred[0.95],
                alpha=0.3, color='blue', label='90% Prediction Interval')

ax.set_title('Probabilistic Forecast with Prediction Intervals', 
             fontsize=14, fontweight='bold')
ax.set_xlabel('Date')
ax.set_ylabel('Passengers (thousands)')
ax.legend()
ax.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('probabilistic_forecast.png', dpi=300, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

# Evaluate coverage
in_interval = (test['Passengers'] >= quantiles_pred[0.05]) & \
              (test['Passengers'] <= quantiles_pred[0.95])
empirical_coverage = in_interval.mean()

print(f"\nProbabilistic Forecast Evaluation:")
print(f"Target coverage: 90%")
print(f"Empirical coverage: {empirical_coverage * 100:.1f}%")
print(f"Mean interval width: {np.mean(quantiles_pred[0.95] - quantiles_pred[0.05]):.2f}")
```

## Phase 9: Production Deployment

### Creating a Production Pipeline

```python
import joblib
import json
from datetime import datetime

class ForecastingPipeline:
    """
    Production-ready forecasting pipeline with versioning and monitoring.
    """
    def __init__(self, model_name):
        self.model_name = model_name
        self.model = None
        self.preprocessors = {}
        self.metadata = {}
        self.version = datetime.now().strftime("%Y%m%d_%H%M%S")
        
    def fit(self, train_data, model, preprocessors=None):
        """
        Train and package model with preprocessing pipeline.
        """
        self.model = model
        self.preprocessors = preprocessors or {}
        
        self.metadata = {
            'model_name': self.model_name,
            'version': self.version,
            'train_start': str(train_data.index[0]),
            'train_end': str(train_data.index[-1]),
            'train_samples': len(train_data),
            'training_date': datetime.now().isoformat(),
            'features': list(train_data.columns) if hasattr(train_data, 'columns') else []
        }
        
        return self
    
    def predict(self, input_data, steps=1):
        """
        Generate forecast with error handling.
        """
        try:
            # Apply preprocessing
            processed_data = input_data.copy()
            for name, transformer in self.preprocessors.items():
                processed_data = transformer(processed_data)
            
            # Generate forecast
            forecast = self.model.predict(processed_data)
            
            return {
                'forecast': forecast,
                'success': True,
                'error': None,
                'model_version': self.version
            }
        
        except Exception as e:
            return {
                'forecast': None,
                'success': False,
                'error': str(e),
                'model_version': self.version
            }
    
    def save(self, path):
        """
        Save complete pipeline to disk.
        """
        package = {
            'model': self.model,
            'preprocessors': self.preprocessors,
            'metadata': self.metadata,
            'version': self.version
        }
        
        joblib.dump(package, f"{path}/model_{self.version}.pkl")
        
        # Save metadata separately for easy access
        with open(f"{path}/metadata_{self.version}.json", 'w') as f:
            json.dump(self.metadata, f, indent=2)
        
        print(f"Model saved: {path}/model_{self.version}.pkl")
        
    @classmethod
    def load(cls, path, version=None):
        """
        Load pipeline from disk.
        """
        if version:
            filename = f"{path}/model_{version}.pkl"
        else:
            # Load most recent version
            import glob
            models = glob.glob(f"{path}/model_*.pkl")
            if not models:
                raise FileNotFoundError(f"No models found in {path}")
            filename = max(models)  # Most recent by name
        
        package = joblib.load(filename)
        
        pipeline = cls(package['metadata']['model_name'])
        pipeline.model = package['model']
        pipeline.preprocessors = package['preprocessors']
        pipeline.metadata = package['metadata']
        pipeline.version = package['version']
        
        print(f"Model loaded: {filename}")
        print(f"Version: {pipeline.version}")
        
        return pipeline

# Package best model for production
production_pipeline = ForecastingPipeline('airline_passenger_forecast')

# Use the ensemble model as final production model
production_pipeline.fit(
    train_data=train_ml,
    model=meta_model,  # or whichever model performed best
    preprocessors={
        'feature_engineering': lambda df: create_time_features(create_lag_features(df, 'Passengers', lags))
    }
)

# Save to disk
import os
os.makedirs('models', exist_ok=True)
production_pipeline.save('models')
```

### Monitoring System

```python
class ForecastMonitoringSystem:
    """
    Monitors forecast performance and triggers retraining.
    """
    def __init__(self, model_pipeline, retrain_threshold=1.5):
        self.pipeline = model_pipeline
        self.retrain_threshold = retrain_threshold  # MAE multiplier for baseline
        self.performance_history = []
        self.baseline_mae = None
        
    def set_baseline(self, validation_errors):
        """Establish baseline performance from validation period."""
        self.baseline_mae = np.mean(np.abs(validation_errors))
        print(f"Baseline MAE set to: {self.baseline_mae:.2f}")
        
    def log_prediction(self, timestamp, actual, predicted):
        """Record forecast and actual outcome."""
        error = actual - predicted
        self.performance_history.append({
            'timestamp': timestamp,
            'actual': actual,
            'predicted': predicted,
            'error': error,
            'abs_error': abs(error)
        })
        
    def check_performance(self, window=30):
        """
        Check if recent performance has degraded significantly.
        """
        if len(self.performance_history) < window:
            return {'should_retrain': False, 'message': 'Insufficient data'}
        
        recent_errors = [p['abs_error'] for p in self.performance_history[-window:]]
        recent_mae = np.mean(recent_errors)
        
        # Check if performance degraded beyond threshold
        if recent_mae > self.baseline_mae * self.retrain_threshold:
            return {
                'should_retrain': True,
                'current_mae': recent_mae,
                'baseline_mae': self.baseline_mae,
                'degradation': (recent_mae / self.baseline_mae - 1) * 100,
                'message': f'Performance degraded by {(recent_mae / self.baseline_mae - 1) * 100:.1f}%'
            }
        
        return {
            'should_retrain': False,
            'current_mae': recent_mae,
            'baseline_mae': self.baseline_mae,
            'message': 'Performance within acceptable range'
        }
    
    def generate_report(self):
        """Generate performance monitoring report."""
        if not self.performance_history:
            return "No performance data available"
        
        df = pd.DataFrame(self.performance_history)
        
        report = {
            'total_predictions': len(df),
            'date_range': f"{df['timestamp'].min()} to {df['timestamp'].max()}",
            'overall_mae': df['abs_error'].mean(),
            'overall_rmse': np.sqrt((df['error'] ** 2).mean()),
            'bias': df['error'].mean(),
            'std_error': df['error'].std()
        }
        
        return report

# Initialize monitoring
monitor = ForecastMonitoringSystem(production_pipeline, retrain_threshold=1.3)

# Set baseline from validation performance
val_errors = val['Passengers'] - rf_grid.best_estimator_.predict(
    create_time_features(create_lag_features(val.copy(), 'Passengers', lags))[feature_cols].fillna(method='ffill')
)
monitor.set_baseline(val_errors)

# Simulate production monitoring on test data
print("\nSimulating production monitoring:")
for idx, row in test.iterrows():
    # In production, this would be real-time as new data arrives
    predicted = ensemble_weighted[test.index.get_loc(idx)]
    actual = row['Passengers']
    
    monitor.log_prediction(idx, actual, predicted)
    
    # Check performance every 6 predictions
    if len(monitor.performance_history) % 6 == 0:
        status = monitor.check_performance(window=6)
        if status['should_retrain']:
            print(f"\n⚠️  ALERT: {status['message']}")
            print(f"   Current MAE: {status['current_mae']:.2f}")
            print(f"   Baseline MAE: {status['baseline_mae']:.2f}")

print("\n" + "="*70)
print("FINAL MONITORING REPORT")
print("="*70)
report = monitor.generate_report()
for key, value in report.items():
    print(f"{key}: {value}")
```

## Key Takeaways and Best Practices

This case study demonstrates a complete professional forecasting workflow that integrates theory and practice. Several critical lessons emerge from this comprehensive analysis.

First, proper exploratory data analysis is non-negotiable. The decomposition and stationarity tests immediately revealed that naive application of models would fail without appropriate transformations. The log transformation stabilized variance while differencing removed trend and seasonality, creating a stationary series suitable for ARIMA modeling.

Second, baseline models establish crucial performance benchmarks. The seasonal naive forecast provided a simple but effective comparison point. Any sophisticated model must demonstrably outperform seasonal naive to justify its complexity. In many real-world scenarios, simple baselines are surprisingly competitive.

Third, no single model dominates all others across all metrics and conditions. SARIMA excelled at capturing seasonal patterns with theoretical foundations. Machine learning models like Random Forest and XGBoost captured complex nonlinear relationships missed by linear models. Ensemble methods combined their complementary strengths, often achieving the best overall performance.

Fourth, probabilistic forecasting provides essential uncertainty quantification. Point forecasts alone are insufficient for decision-making under uncertainty. Prediction intervals inform risk management, inventory buffers, and capacity planning decisions.

Fifth, production deployment requires robust infrastructure beyond model training. Version control ensures reproducibility and enables rollback if new models underperform. Monitoring systems detect performance degradation before it significantly impacts business operations. Automated retraining pipelines keep models current as data distributions evolve.

Finally, communication and interpretation matter as much as technical accuracy. Stakeholders need to understand model assumptions, limitations, and appropriate use cases. Forecast accuracy metrics must align with business objectives, and model insights should inform strategic decisions beyond mere prediction.

---

## Exercises for Students

To reinforce learning, students should complete the following exercises:

1. Implement automated drift detection using the Page-Hinkley test on a streaming simulation of the airline data. Trigger retraining when drift is detected and compare performance to fixed-schedule retraining.

2. Extend the case study to include hierarchical forecasting by splitting passengers into business and leisure travelers (you may simulate this split). Implement reconciliation and compare to independent forecasts.

3. Conduct a thorough A/B test comparing the SARIMA model to the XGBoost model using the interleaved testing framework. Account for temporal correlation in your statistical inference.

4. Implement conformal prediction intervals for the Random Forest model and verify that empirical coverage matches theoretical guarantees on the test set.

5. Design a causal analysis to estimate the impact of a hypothetical price change on passenger demand using synthetic control methodology. Simulate treatment by modifying historical data.

6. Build a production API using FastAPI or Flask that serves forecasts from the deployed model pipeline, includes monitoring hooks, and provides prediction intervals alongside point forecasts.

7. Create a comprehensive dashboard using Plotly Dash or Streamlit that visualizes historical performance, current forecasts, uncertainty intervals, and model monitoring metrics.

## Additional Resources

For deeper understanding, students should consult the following resources:

For foundational time series theory: "Time Series Analysis" by James Hamilton provides comprehensive coverage of ARIMA models, unit roots, and cointegration.

For modern forecasting methods: "Forecasting: Principles and Practice" by Hyndman and Athanasopoulos (freely available online) offers practical guidance with R implementations.

For machine learning approaches: "Hands-On Machine Learning" by Aurélien Géron covers gradient boosting, random forests, and ensemble methods with clear Python examples.

For probabilistic forecasting: "Probabilistic Forecasting and Bayesian Data Assimilation" by Reich and Cotter provides mathematical foundations for uncertainty quantification.

For causal inference: "Causal Inference: The Mixtape" by Scott Cunningham offers accessible introductions to synthetic control and difference-in-differences.

For production ML systems: "Designing Machine Learning Systems" by Chip Huyen covers MLOps, monitoring, and deployment best practices.

---
